## Where the Framing Breaks Down

**The X-25 is not a research aircraft in the sense the rest of this series uses.** It is a civil production
design, sold as plans to amateur builders, bought off the shelf and given a designation. Nothing was
developed. No aerodynamic unknown was resolved. Treating it through a keystone derivation implies a design
process that did not take place, and this article's derivations describe **the concept** rather than an
engineering effort that anyone actually performed on this airframe.

**The keystone was never in doubt.** Autorotation was understood in the 1920s. The X-25 did not test whether
a rotor autorotates, and an article that dimensions the rotor as though it were being designed misrepresents
what the money bought.

**The designation is the anomaly, not the aircraft.** The X-series was created for aircraft built to answer
questions that could not be answered on the ground. The X-25 is a catalogue item with a serial number
applied to it. That is a genuine finding about how the designation system drifted, and it belongs with the
other evidence the closing article of this series will assemble.

**Finally, the comparison this article rests on may be unfair in one direction.** A parachute is compared
against a rotor at equal weight, but the parachute is also the reserve. The rotor does not replace the
parachute in the sequence, it follows it, so the crewman carries both and the mass comparison should
arguably be against the marginal mass of the rotor rather than against the canopy that is carried
regardless. **On that accounting the rotor is worse than this article makes it look**, not better.

## The Source Base

**The record for the aircraft itself is the thinnest in the series so far, and the record for the physics around it is not.**
That asymmetry shapes the whole article and is worth stating plainly, so that a reader does not have to
infer it from the reference list.

**The vehicle itself has essentially no technical literature.** One research record cited in this article carries Bensen in its title, which is the chapter on Igor Bensen and the gyrocopter in a history of the type, [Chapter 11 IGOR Bensen 2003][research_chapter_11_2003], and none carries the X-25 or the Discretionary Descent Vehicle. **Three** concern a flying rotor seat, which are [Barzda 1970][research_barzda_1970] and [Hollrock and Barzda 1972][research_hollrock_barzda_1972] on the SAVER and [Gross and Mawhinney 1970][research_gross_mawhinney_1970] on the AERCAB flying ejection seat, rather than the X-25. There is no flight test
report, no aerodynamic data report and no programme summary in the open literature reached here.
**The specifications in this article come from museum fact sheets and reference compilations, not from primary documents**,
and where those disagree the article says so rather than choosing.

**The supporting physics is abundant and that asymmetry is the point.** Autorotation, the autogiro, momentum
theory, blade element theory, parachutes and escape systems are all well documented from the 1920s onward.
**The article can derive everything about the concept and can verify almost nothing about the aircraft.**

**The research records are listed cluster by cluster under The Contemporary Literature**, including the period reports, the work of the intervening decades and the standard atmosphere on which every number in this article rests.

**The research survey holds 2,707 research records drawn from Crossref, the NASA Technical Reports Server and the Defense Technical Information Center**, of which 828 are report primaries, being 30.6 percent, by the identifier rule set out under The Definition of Primary. **The median publication year is 2005.** Of the 2,672 dated records, 430 were published in 1968 or earlier, the year the X-25 first flew, being 16.1 percent, and 1,151 were published in 2015 or later, being 43.1 percent.

### The Definition of Primary

**Primacy is a property of the document, and this survey derives it from the identifier.** A record is a report primary when its address is on the NASA Technical Reports Server or it carries a digital object identifier under the 10.21236 prefix, which Crossref registers for Defense Technical Information Center reports. **The NACA autogiro and autorotation reports that carry this article's keystone are report primaries under that rule. The rule does not reach the period journal papers**, such as [Glauert 1927][research_glauert_1927] and [de la Cierva 1930][research_de_la_cierva_1930] on the theory of the autogiro, so 30.6 percent is a lower bound on how much of the survey is first-hand.

**The keystone is thin on primary sources.** Autorotation carries 166 research records and 27 of them are report primaries, which is 16.3 percent against 30.6 percent for the survey as a whole. A record is an autorotation record when its title contains a word beginning autorotat, the word auto-rotative, a windmill or windmilling brake or rotor, or a power-off descent or rotor.

**The likely reason is a vocabulary that spans both eras.** Autorotation is a word the 1930s used and the
2020s still use, and on that shared word **the larger and better-indexed modern computational literature
simply outnumbers the period work**. That is an inference about the literature and not a measurement of it.

### Thin Subjects

**Seven subjects that this article's derivations depend on have thin literatures.** Each count below is the number of research records in this survey whose title carries at least one of the listed terms, with a hyphen read as a space. Where a row joins two groups of terms with the word together, a title must carry a term from each group, and where a row adds terms after the words or else, a title carrying one of those terms is counted as well.

| Subject | Title terms counted | Records |
|---|---|---|
| rotor spin-up and prerotation | prerotation, pre-rotation, prerotator, spin-up, rotor starting, starting torque, rotor run-up | 2 |
| stored rotor energy | rotor, blade together with inertia, kinetic energy, stored energy, or else autorotative index, flywheel | 4 |
| the flying rotor seat | SAVER, flying ejection seat, rescue autogyro, rotor seat | 3 |
| gyroplane safety and accidents | gyroplane, autogyro, autogiro, gyrocopter together with safety, accident, crash, airworthiness | 3 |
| the glide of an autorotating rotor | autorotating, autorotation, autorotative, gyroplane, autogyro, autogiro together with glide, gliding | 2 |
| cutting away a parachute | parachute, canopy together with cutaway, cut away, jettison, disconnect | 0 |
| gyroplane and autorotation training | gyroplane, autogyro, autogiro, autorotation together with training, trainer, instructor, test standards | 7 |

**Rotor spin-up and prerotation stands at two records, and neither is the case this article needs.** One concerns the engine loads of prerotating an autogyro rotor, [Czyż et al 2016][research_czyz_usiak_2016], and one the control of the blades while a helicopter rotor is spun up in wind, [Kargaev et al 2025][research_kargaev_korsun_2025]. No title in the survey names the spin-up of a rotor deployed in descent, although the deployable rotors of the SAVER and of the rotary decelerators under The Contemporary Literature must perform one. The word prerotation also names the prerotation of landing gear wheels and of the human trunk. That gap is the reason the spin-up section of this article reasons from energy rather than from measurement.

**Stored rotor energy appears to stand at four records and does not really.** The relevant work exists and is cited, but it sits inside the autorotation and blade-motion literature rather than beside it, because a paper on autorotative landing is a paper about spending exactly that energy. A dedicated heading for it is nearly empty while the subject is well covered. **A thin heading is not the same thing as a thin subject**, and the distinction is easy to get wrong in the direction of claiming a gap that is not there.

One further caution belongs here. The word flywheel also names
**flywheel energy storage for spacecraft and power grids**, meaning containment rings and composite burst
testing, which is a different discipline that happens to store energy in something that spins.
**That literature is a homonym of this subject rather than a part of it**, and no record of it is counted
here.

**The flying rotor seat, the class to which the SAVER belongs, stands at three records**, given above, and **cutting away a parachute stands at no record**, although it is the irreversible step in the deployment sequence.

**Gyroplane safety stands at three records.** They are [Doberentz et al 2020][research_doberentz_geile_2020], [Houston et al 1998][research_houston_spathopoulos_1998] and [Practice for Continued Airworthiness][research_practice_for], and no title among them compares trained with untrained pilots, which is why the training inference under Epistemic State is labelled as an inference.

**The glide of an autorotating rotor stands at two records**, the flight measurement of an autogiro's gliding performance in [Wheatley 1933][research_wheatley_1933] and a controller for steep glide slopes of an unmanned gyroplane in [Wu 2015][research_wu_2015], so the lift-to-drag ratio of 4 on which the range figures rest is assumed rather than taken from a measured X-25 polar.

**Gyroplane and autorotation training stands at seven records**, three of them the practical test standards for rotorcraft pilots and instructors and the rest on autorotation training in helicopters, so the subject the programme's testing addressed is among the thinnest here.

### What the Survey Admits

**A record is admitted only when a person reading its title finds it on this article's subject.** Its 2,707 records come from NASA's Technical Reports Server, the Defense Technical Information Center and the journal literature registered with Crossref. Many words this subject depends on also name something else, and a record that shares only such a word is excluded. Occupant, crash and vehicle also name road traffic safety, landing also names knee and ankle loading in sport, and Parachute is also the name of a cardiac device, a heart valve, a band and a form of executive compensation. Vortex ring also names the quantised vortex rings of superfluid helium, samara also names the Samara Region, and canopy also names the crown of a tree. Autorotation also names the rotation of a telephone screen and of the jaw in surgery, height velocity also names the growth rate of children, and descent also names human gait. Prerotation also names the prerotation of landing gear wheels and tyres, of the human trunk, of pump impellers and of signal constellations in wireless networks, and rotor also names the rotors of electric machines, wave energy turbines, gyroscopes and industrial machinery. Drag coefficient also names the drag of sediment and process particles, of the sea surface in meteorology and of the ether in relativity. Electrical power systems, spaceflight outside the atmosphere, the testing and design of ordnance and the engineering of airfield landing mats are excluded as well. Correction, erratum, clarification, retraction and withdrawal notices, figure, table and supplementary-material records, peer-review reports and journal front matter are excluded too, because a survey counts research works and those are parts of works or editorial events rather than works. A signed comment on a paper, a discussion of it and its author's closure or reply are research works, since each argues about a result, and they are admitted when their subject is, while an unsigned discussion and a referee's report on a manuscript are not. Dictionary entries, advertisements and fiction are excluded for the same reason. The residual cluster admits only flight measurement and performance method, the descent, spinning and recovery of aircraft and other bodies, high-altitude aerodynamics and general aerodynamic texts, and fixed-wing work on supersonic and hypersonic flight, missiles and stores, engines, avionics, structures or operations, with no tie to a rotor, a descent or an escape, is not admitted to it or to any other cluster. The same rule excludes, from every cluster, fixed-wing electric and hybrid-electric aircraft, electric machines, the avionics and the operations and markets of unmanned aircraft and air mobility, and the supersonic drag of bodies and projectiles. It also excludes the landing autonomy, guidance, sensing and path planning of unmanned aircraft even when the vehicle is a rotorcraft, the structures, loads, controls, generators and floating platforms of wind turbines and every vertical-axis turbine, the drive trains, gears, lubrication, engines, ice protection, landing gear and cargo equipment of rotorcraft, the stresses and construction of propeller blades, open rotors and propfans, fixed-wing high lift, landing gear, arresting gear and runway operations, the noise of fixed-wing aircraft and engines and occupational noise exposure, simulator engineering and the administration of training, powered rocket landing, glide weapons, the imaging payloads of parafoils, the network coverage of landing sites, hearing protection and speech reception in aircraft, the quality assurance of manufacture, and airships and balloons. Conference proceedings, symposium volumes, appendices, patent notices and book reviews are excluded as parts of works or notices rather than works. Reference and definition entries such as glossaries, news items and announcements, and a second deposit of a document already counted are not admitted either, because none of them is a separate research work.

**Some records of doubtful relevance are kept.** Injury biomechanics under blunt impact, parachute physiology and equipment, helmet-mounted display optics, bird and insect flight aerodynamics, classical vortex rings in fluid mechanics and combustion, kites and tethered autogyros for airborne wind energy, autorotation used to harvest energy and the drag of projectiles at low speed each share either the physics of a rotor, a descent or a body in flight, or the human side of an escape, with this subject, so each is kept and a reader may weigh it accordingly.

**Every research title has been read for relevance, so the off-topic share that remains is a matter of reading judgement.** A reading of titles cannot show that a work is relevant in substance, so this is a statement about titles only.

## Epistemic State

**Historical fact, from the public record.** Three X-25 type aircraft were ordered in early 1968 under the
Discretionary Descent Vehicle programme. The X-25B, serial 68-10771, flew on 23 January 1968 and the X-25A,
serial 68-10770, on 5 June 1968. Both derive from Bensen B-8 family designs. Testing addressed piloting
technique and training requirements. No full-scale operational test was performed and funding ended with the
American withdrawal from Vietnam. The X-25A is held by the National Museum of the United States Air Force
and the X-25B by the Air Force Flight Test Museum at Edwards. The Kaman SAVER flew towed on 28 December 1971
and with a telescoping rotor on 10 January 1972, and was cancelled.

**Engineering analysis, derived here and checkable.** The equivalence between the autorotation ratio of 1.8
and a disc-referred drag coefficient of 1.23 is algebra containing no fitted quantity. The finding that an
autorotating rotor descends faster than a personnel canopy at equal weight follows from disc loading alone.
The glide range figures follow from an assumed lift-to-drag ratio of 4. The spin-up energy of about 57 feet
of ideal height loss follows from an assumed blade mass and tip speed. The SAVER descent rate of 56 feet per
second follows from documented weight and rotor diameter and is the least assumption-dependent number in the
article. The statement that a rotor at Martian density descends about ten times faster is the same
square-root relation evaluated at one percent of sea-level density and assumes nothing else,
**though it is an illustration of the relation and not a claim about any particular Mars vehicle**, which
would fly a rotor sized for that atmosphere.

**Assumed, and marked as such throughout.** The escape-configuration gross weight of 350 pounds. The
lift-to-drag ratio of 4. The best-glide speed of 45 miles per hour. The blade mass of 12 pounds each and tip
speed of 400 feet per second. The canopy comparison figures of 28 foot nominal diameter, 250 pound suspended
weight and a drag coefficient of 0.75. **None of these is a measurement of an X-25.**

**Inference, and labelled as inference.** That the programme's real finding concerned training rather than
aerodynamics is an inference from the record's statement that the aircraft were used to evaluate piloting
technique and training requirements, combined with the absence of any aerodynamic finding. That the ram-air
parachute is the historical answer to the Discretionary Descent Vehicle question is an inference from
capability comparison, not from any documented decision connecting the two.

**Two further inferences come from the contemporary survey and are labelled here because they are the article's strongest claims and its least documented.**
That a multirotor cannot autorotate follows from the stored-energy and disc-loading relations this article
derives, applied to a configuration those relations describe accurately, but
**no source consulted here states it as a finding and the airworthiness literature treats redundancy as a design choice rather than as a concession**.
And that the gyroplane accident record supports the programme's own conclusion about training is an
inference from the type's general reputation and from the contemporary safety literature,
**not from any study that compares trained and untrained gyroplane pilots**, which would be the evidence the
claim actually wants.

The vehicle-by-vehicle programme record for the X-series generally is [Jenkins, Landis and Miller on the
American X-vehicles][book_jenkins_landis_miller_2003].

**What the record does not settle.** Whether the rotor diameter was 20 feet or 20 feet 6 inches. Whether the
engine was 72 or 90 horsepower. What the third ordered aircraft was and what became of it. Whether the
parachute-cutaway sequence was ever demonstrated at all, as opposed to not being demonstrated at full scale.
Whether any quantitative descent or glide measurement was taken and survives in a closed report.

**Written from current knowledge.** The contemporary survey postdates the editorial date of this article.

## Out of Scope

The detailed aeroelastic behaviour of wooden rotor blades. Ejection seat dynamics and the injury mechanisms
of the ejection itself. The combat search and rescue apparatus the programme was meant to supplement. The
amateur-built rotorcraft accident record, which is substantial and is a different subject. The full history
of the Cierva autogiro. Powered-lift and compound rotorcraft. The detailed design of ram-air canopies. Wind
turbine engineering beyond the identity of the flow state.

## Conclusion

**The Bensen X-25 answered a question nobody had asked clearly enough.**

The programme was framed as giving a descending crewman a choice. The arithmetic says that an autorotating
rotor is a worse decelerator than the parachute it was to replace, by nine percent at equal weight and
twenty-nine percent as flown, and that everything it offers comes from the fact that it is a wing rather
than a brake. **Seven and a half miles from ten thousand feet is the whole product.**

The obvious objection, that a rotor must be spun up while a canopy merely inflates,
**does not survive being written down**. Fifty-seven feet of ideal height loss is cheap. The real difficulty
was never energetic. It was that the sequence requires cutting away a working parachute, and that flying a
gyroplane is a skill, and the programme's own testing went to training requirements rather than to
aerodynamics.

**The question was good and it was eventually answered by fabric.** A ram-air canopy reaches the same glide
ratio with no rigid structure, no spin-up and far less training, and guided airdrop has since removed the
pilot from the loop as well.
**The rotor's physics did not die. It went into wind energy, where the windmill brake state is the design condition rather than an emergency.**

The next article in this series takes up the Schweizer X-26 Frigate.
